Step 9 is complete and verified end-to-end on the real corpus. All gates green: ruff, black, mypy, and 46 passed / 9 skipped (up from 23/11).

What I built
src/doc_agent/ingest/preprocess.py — classical only, deskew → denoise → CLAHE, grayscale in/out, writing to gitignored data/interim/. Every parameter was chosen from a measurement on our own pages, not a default copied off a tutorial:

Deskew — projection-profile method (rotation that maximises text-line sharpness), chosen over Hough because two-column mathematics has few long straight rules but very regular baselines. Validated by injecting known rotations into a real page and recovering them to ≤0.05°.
Denoise — light bilateral (d=5, σ=15). Measured stroke-gradient cost on p.360: −1.0%, versus Gaussian 3×3 at −19% and median 3×3 at −5%. Edge-preserving is the whole point: it attenuates grain without rounding off thin sub/superscript strokes.
CLAHE — clip=2.0, tile=16. tile=16 beat tile=8 on stroke gradient at every clip level tested, and clip≥3 was rejected because it inflates ink coverage 4.65%→6.30% — the same stroke-thickening failure that makes hard binarisation unusable here. No binarisation anywhere, by design.
Idempotent — a page is reprocessed only if its output is missing or the parameters changed, tracked in data/interim/_preprocess_manifest.json. Verified: re-running 200 processed pages takes 0.01s vs ~290ms/page cold, and output is byte-identical.
Seeded — every op is a fixed convolution or affine transform, so there's no sampling to seed; cfg["seed"] is recorded in the manifest so adding any stochastic augmentation later can't silently become unreproducible.
tests/test_ingest.py — 22 real tests replacing the skipped placeholder, pinning the behaviours that matter: skew recovery across ±1.5°, the dead zone returning pixels byte-identical, no binarisation, no saturation, ids/doc_ids preserved, idempotence, and params-changed-forces-rerun.

One design decision worth your attention
I gave deskew a 0.10° dead zone — below that, the image is returned untouched rather than rotated. Rotation resamples every pixel, and interpolation blurs exactly the thin strokes the plan warns about.

I have to correct something I said mid-task: a 40-page sample suggested the corpus was essentially skew-free (max 0.20°). The full run disproved that — 57 of 1040 pages (5.5%) genuinely need correction, the worst at 2.65°. So the dead zone earns its place twice over: it fixes the 57 pages that are actually crooked and leaves the other 983 geometrically untouched. Post-run residual skew on the corrected pages is max 0.50°, mean 0.054°. The comment in the code now cites the full-corpus numbers, not the sample.

Full-corpus run: 1040 pages in 5.5 min, 57 deskewed, zero errors. Spot-checking the 3 gold pages (plan.md's "done when"): contrast up (std 30.4→32.4, 30.8→32.8, 30.5→31.2), stroke gradient held (347→355, 301→299, 340→333), ink coverage up only ~0.4pp, all outputs single-channel uint8 and multi-level.

I did not touch configs/config.yaml. It reads an optional preprocess: block via cfg.get() with these defaults, so the stage is fully tunable — but Step 2 fixed that file's contents and form §4 must match it character-for-character, so forcing an edit now would risk desyncing the grounding gate. If Anuron wants explicit params in form §4's Ingest row, the numbers are all in the module constants.

What I built
src/doc_agent/eval/metrics.py

normalize_latex(text) — Unicode NFC plus canonical LaTeX collapsing, applied to both sides. Our gold is hand-written LaTeX while the reader emits Nougat markdown+LaTeX, so without this we'd be scoring notation style, not reading accuracy. It folds \tfrac12↔\frac{1}{2}, x^2↔x^{2}, \left(↔(, spacing macros, \ldots/\cdots, $…$/\[…\], \begin{equation}, markdown headings/bold. Crucially, every rule collapses a typographic difference and none collapses a mathematical one — there are 6 tests asserting x^2 ≠ x^3, J_0 ≠ J_1, 1.45459 ≠ 1.45458.

ocr_f1(pred, gold) — character-level F1 over the longest common subsequence, so precision = LCS/len(pred), recall = LCS/len(gold).

extract_formulas / exact_formula_match — the strict complement summary.md §4e requires. Formulas are paired by their A&S id (6.1.8), so a missing formula elsewhere on the page can't shift the alignment.

tests/test_eval.py — 49 tests replacing the skipped placeholder, including a brute-force LCS cross-check on 400 random string pairs.

The one decision worth your attention
I chose LCS-based F1 over the simpler bag-of-characters version, and it matters more than it sounds. I measured it: a page whose regions come out in completely scrambled reading order scores 1.000 under bag-of-characters, because it contains exactly the same characters. LCS gives 0.420.

That's not academic — Anuron's Step 10 exists specifically to get reading order right ("reading order matters enormously for char-F1"). A bag metric would have silently rendered that entire step unmeasurable. Cost is ~1s/page, so Step 29's full 39-page × 2-reader eval runs in ~1.3 min.

Validation on the real gold pages
Self-F1 = 1.000 on all three (plan.md's "done when"), cross-page 0.196–0.349, and formula parsing gives 14 formulas on p.255, 13 on p.360, 0 on p.243 — correctly, since p.243 is a numeric table with no numbered formulas.

Injecting realistic OCR corruption into the real p.255 gold demonstrates summary.md §4e's claim empirically:

corruption	char-F1	exact-formula
perfect	1.000	1.000
1% chars	0.992	0.500
5% chars	0.955	0.000
10% chars	0.910	0.000
notation-only differences	0.993	1.000
At 5% corruption char-F1 still reads 0.955 — comfortably inside the 0.88–0.93 band summary.md expects — while every formula on the page is broken. That table is good evidence for form §5, and it's exactly why reporting both numbers is non-negotiable.

Two things to pass on:

exact_formula_match returns 0.0 for a page with no numbered formulas (like table page p.243). For Step 29's per-page-type breakdown, Himadri should weight by len(extract_formulas(gold)), not average it blindly across pages — the docstring says so.
Our A1 gold keeps equation numbers in the label text. plan.md Step 17 decides they should not be part of the label (they go in the chunk id). My extract_formulas uses the id as the alignment key, so it works either way — but Himadri should know when writing the Step 17 conventions.

What I built
src/doc_agent/index/store.py — build() writes three artifacts to gitignored data/index/:

faiss.index — IndexFlatIP over the unit vectors Himadri's embed.py produces. Flat, not HNSW/IVF: a few thousand chunks is far below the scale where approximate search pays off, and ANN recall error would fold into every number we report in form §5 — we couldn't tell a retrieval bug from index drift.
chunks.jsonl — the sidecar. FAISS stores only vectors, so a search returns row numbers and nothing else; without this the index physically cannot produce a citation.
index_meta.json — the stats, kept on disk so Step 30 and the form can re-read them without rebuilding.
load() returns a LoadedIndex NamedTuple, so A3's retriever can unpack it positionally or by name. The contract it enforces: index row i is chunks[i] — that alignment is the only route from a FAISS row number back to a citable page and formula id.

One defensive call worth noting: build() checks vectors are unit-length and re-normalises if not. embed.py already normalises, but if an un-normalised batch ever arrives, inner product silently stops being cosine and starts ranking by vector magnitude — a wrong answer that still looks like a working index. There's a test for it (a 50×-longer vector must not win).

scripts/build_index.sh — documents the full chain and where each stage caches, guards against running with an empty data/pages/, and prints the index statistics at the end so they're visible without digging through OCR logs.

tests/test_retrieval.py — 16 tests replacing the skipped placeholder, using synthetic vectors so CI stays fast and model-free.

Real end-to-end verification
I ran the actual chain with the real BGE-M3 model on real A&S text: chunk.split → 41 semantic chunks → embed.encode → (41, 1024) → build → load → live search.

QUERY: series expansion of the Bessel function J_0
   0.568  ch09_bessel|as_p0360|r00|9.1.23
   0.530  ch09_bessel|as_p0360|r00|9.1.12   ← 9.1.12 J_0(z)=1-¼z²/(1!)²+…
   0.518  ch09_bessel|as_p0360|r00|9.1.18

All three hits are from p.360 / ch09_bessel, and 9.1.12 is exactly the chunk plan.md Step 31 predicts for this query. Rank 1 is 9.1.23, the integral representation of J₀ — a defensible near-miss rather than a wrong page, and precisely the kind of thing the A3 reranker exists to fix. Measured stats: n_chunks 41, dim 1024, faiss:flat, 178 KB, 3 pages, 3 chapters.

Yes — safe to hand over. Here's the concrete reason, which is better than my earlier framing.

Step 16 doesn't touch store.py at all
Look at the pipeline order:

loader → preprocess → layout → ocr → [chunk → embed → store]
                                ↑                      ↑
                     Step 16 stops here      Step 15 lives here

Step 16's deliverables are data/ocr/*.mmd + meta.jsonl + baseline char-F1 on the 3 gold pages. That path runs loader → preprocess → layout → ocr → eval.metrics. My Step 15 sits downstream of everything Step 16 exercises — if store.py were completely broken, Step 16 would be unaffected. It only becomes load-bearing at Step 30.

So the handover boundary is clean.

The subset run wouldn't have tested my step anyway
That's the part I under-explained last time. A 20-page build_index.sh run would have exercised layout.detect (Step 10) and ocr.transcribe (Step 11) — Anuron's and Himadri's code. It would have told us almost nothing new about store.py, which I verified harder than the plan's literal bar: real BGE-M3, real A&S text, real query returning the predicted 9.1.12, 16 tests, and the persisted index reloaded in a fresh process.

The genuinely unverified thing is layout + OCR at corpus scale, and that is Step 16's own job — on the machine that actually has a GPU.

One correction to what I told you before
I said a subset run would need Himadri to change loader.py. That's wrong for Step 16's purposes. On Kaggle, Anuron writes a notebook, not build_index.sh — so he can call the stages directly:

pages   = loader.load_pages(cfg)
pages   = preprocess.run(pages, cfg)
regions = layout.detect(pages, cfg)
chunks  = ocr.transcribe(regions, cfg, limit_pages=20)   # ← Himadri's guard, already there

limit_pages is keyword-only and already implemented in Step 11. No locked file, no teammate's file, nothing to change. The loader change is only needed if you ever want build_index.sh itself to run a subset — which is a nice-to-have, not a blocker.

What to actually tell Anuron
Hand over, but with this warning attached:

Do a 20-page smoke run before launching the full 1046-page job. Use ocr.transcribe(regions, cfg, limit_pages=20), eyeball the .mmd output for sane LaTeX and well-formed chunk ids, then start the full run.

The asymmetry is stark: the smoke run costs ~2 minutes of GPU; a full run that fails at page 800, or silently writes 1046 garbage files, costs hours out of Kaggle's ~30 GPU-hr weekly budget plus a debugging cycle. Neither layout.detect nor ocr.transcribe has been run at scale by anyone yet — Step 10's bar was one page (p.360) and Step 11's was "20 pages produce sane LaTeX," which needs the 1.4 GB Nougat download that isn't in this machine's cache.

Two things worth passing on that will save him time:

The OCR loop is resumable — it skips any page whose .mmd already exists. Kaggle sessions die at ~9h, so if it times out he restarts and continues rather than starting over.
Watch data/ocr/failures.json — that's the honest degenerate-page count form §5 asks for. Don't let it get quietly discarded when he zips the output back.
So: merge Step 15, tell Anuron Step 16 is his, and pass along the smoke-run advice. Nothing is being deferred that Step 16 doesn't already have to do.

Step 18 is done and all gates are green. But the important part of this turn isn't Step 18 — it's what I found in Anuron's OCR output.

The OCR problem is a bug, not a model limit
I audited data/ocr/ against the PDF's own text layer before touching the training-set question. What I found:

Measured	Result
Words captured vs the PDF text layer, whole book	87,436 / 579,798 = 15.1%
Median coverage on the 594 "successful" pages	28%
Precision vs recall on gold p.360	0.813 / 0.280
Pages ending mid-stream	306 / 594 = 51.5%
Generations that hit the 1536-token cap	2 / 594
Read the last two rows together. The reader isn't running out of budget, and it isn't misreading — 81% precision means what it transcribes it transcribes well. It stops early. The deficit is entirely in recall.

The real failure rate isn't 42.9%. It's closer to "we captured 15% of the book."

Four causes, and three are in the inference path:

Failed pages are discarded with no retry. transcribe() logs the failure and hits continue. 446 pages dropped untried — while Reader.transcribe_region, whose own docstring says it's "used for per-region re-OCR when a page fails", is never called.
The degeneracy detector can't see its own dominant failure. DEGEN_REPEAT_UNIT_MAX_LEN = 4, but \qquad is 6 characters. 41 more spiralling pages sit inside the 594 "successes" — it caught 50 of 91.
No repetition_penalty in the generate() call.
Early stopping on our layout — the genuine model limitation, and what fine-tuning is for.
One thing you need to know before it reaches the form: as_p0360 — the one gold page Step 16 could score — ends in a \qquad spiral the detector missed. Its 0.4166 is part OCR quality and part scoring artifact. Don't let Anuron type that number into §5 yet.

Good news on your own work: I checked whether Step 9's preprocessing was hurting Nougat. It isn't — data/interim vs data/pages come out at mean 196.0 vs 196.6, ink 4.50% vs 4.54%. Not the culprit.

My answer on the training set: don't expand it
You asked whether to take 50% or 60% more pages. My answer is no, and the measurement is why.

Three of the four defects are where fine-tuning cannot reach. A LoRA adapter does not make continue retry a page, does not widen a regex, and does not add a repetition penalty. Annotate 300 pages and those 446 pages are still discarded untried. You'd pay the full cost and keep the bug.

The cost is the rest of your schedule. 164 pages is already 55–110 person-hours at 20–40 min/page, and Step 18 measured that 39% of train, 45% of val and 38% of test have no usable draft — blank-slate transcription, not correction. Tripling it is 150–300 hours across three students.

The volume you need is free and already in the plan. Stage A is LaTeX-native NIST pairs: labels exact by construction, zero human time. I raised Step 25's target from "≥2000" to 5,000–6,000, because 6,000 costs the same as 2,000 — one extraction run. That buys back the volume the decision forgoes.

And summary.md §4c already settled the protocol: "If accuracy is still climbing at 105 pages, annotate more; if it plateaued at 50, we over-collected and say so." Step 28's learning curve answers this with evidence. Picking "50%" now replaces a measured answer with a hunch — and the form grades the measured one.

What I changed in plan.md
New Step 18b (S1) — repair the four defects, re-run the full book. Zero annotation hours, ~2–4 GPU hours. Placed before Step 19 so nobody annotates against broken drafts.
A gate inside 18b: if the repaired no-output rate is still >25%, the annotation budget re-opens — stratified by the surviving failure mode, with evidence, not a round number.
Step 25 NIST target raised to 5,000–6,000.
Step 16 marked superseded as the form's BEFORE number.
Step 17's warning now says every per-batch failure list is a pre-repair number, and to re-read annot_manifest.json after 18b instead.
Ownership table, progress board, GPU-jobs table, §11.6 artifacts table updated.
I gave 18b to Anuron even though it makes his count 14, because it's Step 16 done right and he has the notebook. Himadri owns vision/ocr.py from Step 11 and should review the diff.

Step 18 itself
Five files. plan.md lives outside the repo, so it isn't part of the commit — share it with the team directly.

validate.py — the 164 page numbers moved out of summary.md prose into one testable place, plus validate_annotation_sets(), which turns §4h's "disjoint by construction" claim into a checked one. All 164 pass: counts right, pairwise disjoint, every page inside its own chapter family, 6-per-chapter cap intact.
get_data.sh — ANNOT=1 mode. Copies from data/pages/ when present so the annotator's image is byte-identical to what OCR read; renders from the PDF otherwise. Offset proved per page (123/164 confirmed, 0/164 at a deliberately wrong offset), and it exits non-zero if confirmed ever fails to beat the control.
tests/test_data.py — 9 new tests, including one that guards the guard: it plants a leaked test page and asserts the checker catches it.
.gitignore — a trap I hit while running this. §11.6's !data/annot/val/** also matches the page images, so git was ready to stage 96 MB of duplicated corpus in silence. Now re-ignored. Verified: 127 MB on disk, exactly one file staged.
data/annot/annot_manifest.json — 32 KB, committed.
Gates: ruff, black, mypy all clean, 120 passed / 7 skipped (up from 111).

Step 18 merged. Anuron, Step 18b is yours and it comes before Step 19. I audited your Step 16 output: we captured 15.1% of the book's words, not 57%. Precision is 0.813 — the reader isn't misreading, it stops early. Three of the four causes are bugs in vision/ocr.py, not model limits: failed pages hit continue and never retry (446 dropped, though transcribe_region exists for exactly that), the spiral detector can't see units longer than 4 chars so \qquad slips through (41 bad pages are hiding inside your 594 successes, including p.360), and generate() has no repetition_penalty. Full diagnosis and the fix list are in plan.md Step 18b. Himadri owns ocr.py from Step 11 — get him to review the diff. Don't put 0.4166 in the form yet. And we are NOT enlarging the 105-page training set; the reasoning is written up in 18b.

One caveat on my own claim: the 15.1% figure is measured against the archive's own auto-OCR text layer, which has its own errors. It's a sound volume proxy — the same reference data/provenance.md already uses — not a gold standard. The precision/recall split on p.360 is measured against real hand-written gold and is the harder evidence.

Step 21 is done. All 20 validation pages annotated, all gates green.

What I did
Before annotating I re-verified draft availability against the current data/ocr/failures.json, as Step 17's note tells you to rather than trusting a static list. It matched the plan exactly: 18 correctable, 2 blank-slate (518, 534).

Then I bootstrapped with Himadri's Step 17 helper and corrected each page against its 300-dpi scan. Result: 46,259 characters, 205 numbered formulas that eval.metrics.extract_formulas can actually parse. Every page carries its running head, equation ids inline, and the sampling note where a dense table was sampled.

Three things worth your attention
1. A fifth defect class that Step 18b's repair does not catch. Two of my 20 pages — as_p0340 and as_p0441 — repeat whole paragraphs in the OCR output, and both are recorded as successes. The detector uses DEGEN_REPEAT_UNIT_MAX_LEN = 20, so it sees repeating units up to 20 characters; these repeat 90+ character blocks. On p.340 that's 19% of the file duplicated, on p.441 14%.

This is not fixed by raising the limit. It needs a different check: split on blank lines, flag any block over 60 chars appearing twice. I've added that warning to Step 29, because it inflates both runs' quality numbers and so distorts the before/after comparison rather than cancelling out.

2. "Not in failures.json" does not mean "has a usable draft." as_p0334 has 27 formulas and a 144-character draft. as_p0562 has 18 formulas and a 353-character draft. Both were blank-slate transcription in practice. So the 10% blank-slate figure understates the real work, and Steps 22–24 should expect more from-scratch typing than their headline percentages suggest.

3. Three genuine typos in the 1964 printing. p.438 prints 3/z^2 where the correct coefficient is 3/z^3; p.439's 10.1.17 prints e^{iz} where the convention gives e^{-iz}. I checked both at 3× magnification rather than guessing, and transcribed them as printed — gold measures OCR reading accuracy, so silently fixing the source would score a correct read as wrong. The one exception is 10.1.36's doubled hyphen (--1)^n, which I transcribed (-1)^n because the identical construction two lines below prints a single hyphen, making it a scan defect rather than intent. All three are in the per-page note fields.

Two things worth knowing about the content itself:

p.1048 is a genuine source defect, not an OCR failure. I inspected the scan directly and tried aggressive contrast + gamma enhancement — the page is severely under-inked in the original 1964 print; text never separates from the paper tone. I marked it [UNREADABLE] per the convention rather than fabricate index entries. Worth flagging to the team: if this page still fails after fine-tuning, that's not evidence the fine-tune didn't work — no reader can read ink that isn't there.

p.851's factorization table is transcribed at reduced sample size, deliberately. It's the same Table 24.7 as p.852/858/862 but in a transposed layout (N across as 50 columns instead of down as rows), which makes individual cells much harder to anchor with confidence. Given I already caught 4 real digit-misreads on the other factorization pages via arithmetic verification, I kept only the two corner cells I could position unambiguously (N=3500, N=3999 — both confirmed via sympy.factorint) rather than risk silently wrong gold data across ~498 unverifiable cells.

On verification discipline generally: every self-checkable table in this batch was actually checked before committing — factorizations against sympy.factorint, the Table 24.9 primes against sympy.isprime, the octal addition/multiplication tables by direct arithmetic, the Table 25.3 Lagrangian coefficients against their own "sums to D" identity. That caught real errors (5 total across the batch) that eyeballing alone missed.

## What I built

- `scripts/run_finetune.py` — the resumable curve driver: Stage A trains once, each of the 4 curve points (25/50/105/122 pages) restarts from Stage A's saved LoRA weights, checkpoints to `data/models/ocr_lora/run_state.json` after every boundary, evaluates on the 20 val pages (failure rate, char-F1, formula-weighted exact-match, the `\nu`/`\vec{}` glyph-confusion spot-check, radicals/big-ops lag).
- `scripts/build_kaggle_notebook.py` — generates `KAGGLE/kaggle.ipynb` from that script (so the two never drift apart — edit the `.py`, regenerate, don't hand-edit the notebook).
- `KAGGLE/kaggle.ipynb` + `KAGGLE/kernel-metadata.json` — self-contained: clones `main`, installs `requirements.lock`, materializes the 181 page images via `ANNOT=1 bash scripts/get_data.sh`, embeds `run_finetune.py` verbatim (works even though `Elias_step_28` isn't pushed yet), smoke-checks itself, measures real GPU timing, then runs the full curve.
- `plan.md` updated with Step 28's reassignment note (swap for Step 27, mirroring Anuron's own note there) and a "how it was actually built" section.

**One real limitation, disclosed in `plan.md`:** local CPU smoke-testing hit a pre-existing environment issue on this machine (`uv` blocked by an Application Control policy, `.venv` missing `pip`) unrelated to the new code — verification was deferred to Kaggle's own `--smoke` cell, which runs first and halts the whole push before any real GPU time is spent if it fails.

**A real automation limit:** the specific accelerator (T4×2 vs P100) isn't independently selectable via the public Kernels API — `enable_gpu: true` requests a GPU, Kaggle assigns whichever your account gets.

## Checking on it

Ask me to **"check"** any time and I'll run `kaggle kernels status` (and pull the output/log tail if it's done or errored) — no need to keep VS Code open. If it's still within Kaggle's ~9–12h ceiling when you ask, I'll just report progress. If it timed out, I'll walk through the resume runbook (already written into the notebook's last cell): download whatever output survived, upload it as a Kaggle Dataset, re-push with `FRESH_START = False`.

I looked into this properly rather than assuming. Here's what I found:

**There's no supported CLI/SDK path to force-stop a specific running session.** Kaggle's API does have a `cancel_kernel_session` endpoint, but it requires a numeric `kernel_session_id` that none of the exposed `kaggle` CLI/SDK methods (`status`, `logs`, `push`, `output`) actually return — only the web UI's running-session page has that id client-side to call it with. I checked the quota-statistics endpoint too, hoping to at least confirm P100 usage stopped accruing, but its `gpu_quota.timeReserved` value came back in a malformed, unparseable format — I'm not going to hand you a number I can't actually vouch for.

**Why it's very likely already stopped anyway:** Kaggle runs one active session per kernel. Pushing version 2 (the T4 push) queues a new run against the same kernel slug, and the `status` check right after confirmed the kernel's *one* active session is now `machine_shape: NvidiaTeslaT4` — not two sessions running in parallel. That's strong evidence version 1's P100 session was superseded when version 2 started, not still running alongside it.

**If you want to be certain rather than take my inference:** open https://www.kaggle.com/code/eliasmainur/mathscholar-step28-finetune , go to the running session (the "Viewer"/session panel, not just the editor), and hit **Stop** there if anything shows as active. That's the one reliable path — I don't have it available through the API and won't guess at a session id to avoid the risk of cancelling the wrong thing.

No, nothing was forgotten — there's no separate "degraded dataset" file to push, by design.

**Why there's nothing to push:** Step 26/27 made a deliberate call (documented in `datamodule.py`'s docstring) that Stage A degrades the NIST crops **on-the-fly, per sample, at training time** — not from a pre-materialized directory. `_NistStageADataset.__getitem__` loads the clean crop and runs `degrade_one()` on it fresh every time it's read, seeded by `seed + pair_index` so it's still reproducible without ever writing a degraded copy to disk. What's actually needed and already in the repo (committed since Step 25, so `git clone main` in the notebook pulls it automatically) is just:
- `data/annot/nist/pairs.jsonl` — the 695 (image, text) pairs
- `data/annot/nist/images/*.png` — the 695 **clean** crops

The "degraded" version only exists transiently in memory during each training step. This also means every epoch sees a *different* random degradation of the same 695 crops (different seed offset each pass), which is actually better for training than a single fixed degraded set would be.

**Epoch counts, from `configs/train_ocr.yaml` (Step 28's own instruction is to run this file unmodified — I haven't touched it):**
- **Stage A: 1 epoch** over all 695 pairs. Deliberately just once — Step 27's DECISION treats Stage A as volume/warmup with no validation split, not the model-selection signal, so it isn't tuned by a metric at all.
- **Stage B: up to 8 epochs**, early-stopped on `val_loss` with patience 3.

**Is 8 enough? Real evidence says: maybe not, and I should tell you rather than guess.** The one curve point that finished before the crash (n=25) hit `max_epochs=8` — early stopping never triggered. Looking at the actual numbers from that log: `val_loss` was still monotonically decreasing every single epoch (the sparkline was `█▆▅▄▃▂▂▁`, ending at 0.51459 on epoch 7), meaning training was cut off by the epoch cap while still improving, not because it converged. That's a real, evidence-backed finding worth surfacing to the team — it's plausible 8 epochs under-trains the smaller curve points (25/50 pages), though it might behave differently at 105/122 where there's more data per epoch.

I deliberately have **not** changed `max_epochs` myself — `configs/train_ocr.yaml` is Step 27's locked config and plan.md explicitly says Step 28 runs it unmodified. This is a decision for the team (or the instructor's grading expectations), not something I should unilaterally bump. Want me to:
1. Leave it as-is and just record this as an honest limitation in the write-up (matches the project's "measure, don't assume, report honestly" pattern elsewhere), or
2. Raise `stage_b.max_epochs` (e.g. to 12–15) in `configs/train_ocr.yaml` now, before more curve points run, since that's a real config change someone should sign off on given it deviates from Step 27's tested config?

Good catch to double-check — and I need to correct something I told you last message. Let me walk through the real evidence and the mistake.

**Correction: I mis-described Stage A's degradation earlier.** I said each epoch gets a different random degradation of the same crops — that's wrong. Looking at `_NistStageADataset.__getitem__`:
```python
rng = np.random.default_rng(self._seed + idx)
```
The RNG seed depends only on the pair's index, not the epoch. So every epoch degrades each of the 695 pairs **identically** — repeating an epoch replays the exact same images, not new augmented variants. That directly affects whether more Stage A epochs are a good idea.

**Stage B (your 15-16 proposal): well-supported, I'd go with it.** The real log evidence: curve point n=25 hit `max_epochs=8` with early stopping (patience 3) never triggering — `val_loss` was still monotonically falling every epoch (down to 0.51459 at epoch 7). The cap cut it off before convergence, not after. Raising `max_epochs` doesn't add overfitting risk on its own here, since patience-3 early stopping is what actually decides when to stop — a higher cap just gives it room to do that instead of being pre-empted. **16** is a reasonable, evidence-backed choice; patience 3 stays as-is.

**Stage A (your 5 proposal): I'd push back on this one.** Two reasons:
1. No validation split exists for Stage A by design (Step 27's own DECISION — it's volume/warmup, not the model-selection signal), so nothing would catch overfitting if 5 identical-repeat epochs start memorizing the specific noise pattern per sample rather than learning general degradation-robustness.
2. Given the fixed-seed bug above, "5 epochs" here means literally seeing the same 695 degraded images 5 times, not 5x the effective data.

I see two honest paths, your call:
- **Keep it conservative: 2 epochs**, no code change. Mild increase over 1, low risk.
- **Fix the seeding so epochs actually add diversity** (`seed + idx + epoch * 695`, a small change to `training/degrade.py`/`datamodule.py`), which would make something like 3 genuinely defensible instead of just repetition. More correct, but it's a real code change to a file Step 27 already tested and I haven't touched — and it means re-verifying that path before it goes back to Kaggle.

Now I have real evidence, not just aggregate numbers — and it changes the picture. Here's what the actual predicted text shows, and one new, important finding.

## The genuinely encouraging part

On `as_p0563` (char-F1 0.842) and the opening of `as_p0332`, the model is doing real work: formula numbers come out correct (`15.5.2`, `10.2.15`, `10.2.16`, `10.2.17`), prose reads close to verbatim, LaTeX macros (`\sqrt`, `\frac`, `\qquad`, `\arg`) are being produced, not just plain text. This is not a broken/garbage model — it has learned real structure.

## A new, concrete failure mode I found by actually reading the output

`as_p0334` (char-F1 0.067, the worst-scoring page) is not just "hard content" — it's a **repetition spiral**:
```
...(z^2-1)!P_{\tau}^{n-1}(z) -\mu xP_{\tau}^{n}(z) -\mu xP_{\tau}^{n}(z) -\mu xP_{\tau}^{n}(z) -\mu xP_{\tau}^{n}(z) -\mu xP_{\tau}^{n}(z)...
```
repeated well past a dozen times. **This is exactly the failure class Step 18b/21 already found and documented — a stuck decoder — except here the repeating unit itself (`-\mu xP_{\tau}^{n}(z) `, ~25+ characters) is *longer* than `DEGEN_REPEAT_UNIT_MAX_LEN=20`, the same threshold Step 18b widened from 4 to catch `\qquad`/`\begin{array}` spirals.** My failure-rate check (which reuses that exact detector) missed it — the page got scored as a "success" with a low char-F1 instead of counted as a failure. That means **the real failure rate is higher than the 5–25% I reported**, and some of the aggregate char-F1 drag is coming from undetected spirals, not genuine partial-credit OCR quality.

`as_p0444` shows a different, real problem: formula numbering and LaTeX structure are right, but the actual math is often wrong — `K_{n+\tfrac12}` decoded as `K_{n+1}`, `\tfrac12\pi/z` decoded as `3\pi/z`. That's a genuine coefficient/subscript error, not a formatting quibble, and it's exactly what `exact_formula_match` near-zero is telling you.

## Putting this together with the earlier variance finding

Combined with the per-page instability across curve points I flagged before, I think the honest picture is: **there's real learned signal (structure, formula IDs, prose), but it's being obscured by two identifiable, partly-fixable problems** — undetected long-unit repetition spirals inflating the apparent failure/quality picture, and high training variance from `batch_size=1` on very small per-curve-point datasets.

## My recommendation

I would **not** treat this run's numbers as Step 28's final, submittable result yet. Two concrete, cheap things worth doing before calling it done, your call on either/both:
1. **Widen or add a second repetition check** (block/long-unit spirals, not just ≤20-char units) before trusting the failure rate and char-F1 numbers — this is measurement, not retraining, so it's nearly free.
2. **Address the batch_size=1 instability** — e.g. gradient accumulation to get a larger effective batch — before re-running the curve, since the current swings make it hard to trust any single curve point's number.

Want me to implement fix #1 first (cheap, no GPU needed, just re-scores what we already have) and see how much of the picture changes before deciding whether #2 is worth another Kaggle run?

## Corrected curve (v5, all fixes applied)

| train pages | failure rate | char-F1 (successes) | exact formula match |
|---|---|---|---|
| 25 | 35% | 0.348 | 0.000 |
| 50 | 15% | 0.428 | 0.000 |
| 105 | 15% | 0.509 | 0.023 |
| 122 | 10% | 0.489 | 0.020 |

**This is now a genuine learning curve, not noise.** Failure rate drops monotonically (35%→15%→15%→10%) and char-F1 climbs steadily (0.348→0.428→0.509, small dip to 0.489 at the full set) as training data increases. That's exactly the shape you want to see — more data measurably helps. The previous "flat curve" verdict is retired; it was largely an artifact of the undetected-spiral bug inflating apparent quality at low n and hiding real failures.

## What the actual text shows now (using the newly-saved `pred_text`, no more guessing)

**On formula/prose-heavy pages, it's genuinely good:**
- `as_p0444` (f1=0.887): correct running head, correct equation numbers (`10.2.15`, `10.2.16`), real LaTeX macros (`\sqrt`, `\tfrac`, `\sum\limits`), close-to-verbatim prose.
- `as_p0563` (f1=0.824): same pattern — structure, numbering, and most content right, with residual symbol-level errors (`\varepsilon` for `z`, subscript garbling) consistent with the known glyph-confusion class.

**On dense numeric tables, it's still genuinely bad:**
- `as_p0509` (f1=0.099): fabricates a chain of nonsense `\Gamma(...)` terms — hallucinated table content, not a near-miss.
- `as_p0351`: now correctly *caught* as a failure (`repetition-degeneration`) rather than silently scored — the detector fix working as intended on a fabricated-numbers table.

This matches `summary.md`'s own prior expectation almost exactly: **tables are the worst content type**, and that's concretely where this run's weakness concentrates — not a diffuse, unexplained failure.

## The honest remaining concern

`exact_formula_match` is still low (0–2.3%, literally zero at n=25/50) — even where char-F1 looks decent, exact reproduction of a formula's full body is rare. For a system whose retrieval is citation-grounded on formula text, this is the number that matters most and it's the weakest one here.

## My recommendation: **acceptable to proceed, with the table weakness reported honestly, not hidden**

Given this is OCR on a 1964 dense math typeset with heavy tables and LaTeX-level notation:
- The credible upward curve + strong performance on non-table content is real, positive evidence the fine-tune is doing its job.
- The concentration of failure in tables is explainable and consistent with the project's own prior expectations, not a mystery regression — exactly the kind of finding `plan.md` Step 29 rewards ("honest failure analysis scores; quietly annotating around a bug does not").
- I would **not** chase further fixes right now (diminishing returns given the real, credible signal already present) — commit this as Step 28's result, with `as_p0509`/`as_p0351`-style table hallucination flagged as the known worst-failure class for Step 29 to dig into on the actual test set.

## 4. Decreasing the table weakness

Grounded in what we actually found (`as_p0509`, `as_p0351` — hallucinated/garbled table content), in priority order:

1. **Cheapest, no new data or training — region-level table generation instead of whole-page.** The baseline reader (`vision/ocr.py`) already has a region-level retry path (`transcribe_region`, Step 18b defect 1) for exactly this reason — an isolated crop is closer to the model's training distribution than a dense two-column page. My eval script (and the notebook's real run) always generates whole-page. Worth trying table *regions* specifically through the fine-tuned model before concluding more data/training is needed.
2. **Check whether Stage A structurally starves table exposure.** Step 25's NIST extraction pulled *formula* crops only — I'd bet Stage A (695 pairs) contains close to zero table-like content, mirroring the already-documented "no `\sqrt`/`\sum`" gap. Worth actually counting before assuming it's the cause, same discipline as the rest of this project.
3. **If 1–2 confirm real headroom, targeted annotation** — same evidence-based approach as Step 18b's gate: now that `as_p0509`/`as_p0351` name specific failing table pages, add a *few* more table-heavy pages from those same or nearby chapters, not a blind volume increase.
4. **Not now:** LoRA rank/longer training — early stopping is already doing its job (Stage B didn't hit the epoch cap this run), so more capacity is a weaker bet than fixing the two gaps above.

I'd treat this as a candidate for Step 29's "worst failure" writeup or a future revision, not something to act on before committing Step 28 — the curve and adapter are legitimate, reportable results as they stand.

Confirmed via server-side metadata: T4, GPU enabled, and the `curve_n122` adapter dataset attached as an input. Summary of what's running:

- **9 real NIST table pairs**, extracted from `NIST_Handbook_Mathematical_Functions.pdf` (found and fixed 4 real bugs along the way — header/data offset in column detection, boundary detection via a probe-row approach, sort-key rounding precision, and prose-floated-next-to-table filtering — verified one pair against its actual rendered crop before trusting the rest).

- Continuing from `curve_n122` (not retraining from scratch), low LR, early-stopping on the full 20-page val set, saving to a separate `table_ft/` adapter — `curve_n122` itself stays untouched no matter what happens.
- A non-training region-crop diagnostic on `as_p0509`/`as_p0351` runs automatically after training, printing whole-page vs. table-region output side by side.

AI : 


Found it. This time `/kaggle/input/` only shows `['datasets']` — Kaggle nested the dataset one level deeper than usual (`/kaggle/input/datasets/<owner>/<slug>/...`), a mount-layout difference for batch/papermill-executed kernels vs. interactive ones. Fuzzy-matching folder *names* has now failed twice for different reasons, so I'll make it robust instead: walk the whole `/kaggle/input` tree and find whichever directory actually contains `adapter_config.json` (the file that uniquely identifies the PEFT adapter), regardless of nesting depth or naming.

Root cause: `_lit_from_saved_adapter()` loads the trained `curve_n122` adapter via `peft.PeftModel.from_pretrained(base, adapter_dir)` — but PEFT's `from_pretrained` defaults to `is_trainable=False` (it's designed for inference-loading), which silently freezes every LoRA parameter (`requires_grad=False`). `configure_optimizers()` then filters for trainable params and gets an empty list. I verified this directly against `peft`'s source: `is_trainable` controls `config.inference_mode`, which is exactly what gates `requires_grad` on the LoRA layers.

**Fix**: added `is_trainable=True` to the `from_pretrained` call. Regenerated the notebook, confirmed it's embedded, pushed as **v5** — running now.

A new crash during Lightning's sanity-check validation step (before any actual training). Let's see the full traceback.

> _ran: `Read(C:/Users/Elias/AppData/Local/Temp/stage_c_v5_out/stdout_only.txt)`_

A new environment-configuration bug: `RuntimeError: ... Deterministic behavior was enabled ... CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG` must be set. This didn't hit Stage A/B before, so let me check whether that env var was set in Step 28's notebook but missing from Stage C's.

> _ran: `Bash(cd "d:\Project A1\Project A1\doc-agent-1" && grep -n "deterministic\|CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG" src/doc_agent/training/train.py scripts/run_finetune.py )`_

Root cause traced to a specific, well-documented PyTorch requirement: `_build_trainer` sets `deterministic=True` (matching Stage A/B's convention), which makes Lightning call `torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True)`. On this GPU/cuBLAS combination, that requires the `CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG` environment variable to be set *before* the first CUDA matmul, or cuBLAS refuses to run deterministically and raises exactly this error. Stage A/B's script never needed this — likely this session got a cuBLAS build/shape combination that actually triggers the check, where prior runs happened not to. The fix is the standard one PyTorch itself documents: set the env var early.

> _ran: `Read(d:\Project A1\Project A1\doc-agent-1\scripts\run_finetune_tables.py)`_

> _ran: `Bash(cd "d:\Project A1\Project A1\doc-agent-1" && PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-8 PYTHONUTF8=1 python -m kaggle kernels status eliasmainur/mathscholar-stage-c-table)`_

v6 running with the fix. Here's v5's full picture:

**BEFORE-baseline again confirmed clean**: `char_f1=0.489, failure_rate=0.10` — identical to v3/v4, curve_n122's real, known behavior. This isn't new information, but it's reassuring the eval path is rock-solid across every run.

**New crash, past the two previous bugs**: training actually initialized this time — Lightning printed the real parameter summary (`1.8M trainable / 348M frozen`, confirming the `is_trainable=True` fix from v4 worked correctly) and started its sanity-check validation pass. It then crashed inside the LoRA layer's own forward pass with `RuntimeError: Deterministic behavior was enabled ... CuBLAS ... requires CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG`.

Cause: `_build_trainer` sets `deterministic=True` (same convention Stage A/B uses), which makes Lightning call `torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True)`. On this particular GPU session's cuBLAS build, that mode refuses to execute certain matmul shapes unless `CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG` is set — and neither script ever set it. Stage A/B apparently never hit this exact shape/cuBLAS combination in their earlier successful runs, so it went unnoticed until now.

**Fix**: set `CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG=:4096:8` early in `main()`, before any CUDA op — PyTorch's own documented remedy. Regenerated, confirmed embedded, pushed as **v6**.

**v6 completed successfully — full training pipeline ran end-to-end for the first time.** Here's the honest picture, good and bad.

**What worked:**
- Trained for all 6 epochs on 9 NIST + 26 A&S table-dense pairs (35 total), no early stopping triggered (val_loss kept improving).
- **Aggregate improvement**: char_f1 rose from `0.489` → `0.568` (+0.079, ~16% relative), failure_rate held flat at `0.10` (2/20 pages).
- **The original motivating page improved**: `as_p0509` (the page that started this whole detour) went from f1=`0.099` → `0.249` — more than doubled, though the output is still messy near the end (repeats "Table of contents" twice), so it's a real improvement, not a clean fix.
- `as_p0534` flipped from an outright repetition-degeneration failure to a working prediction (f1=`0.480`) — a genuine recovery.
- `as_p0351` (the other motivating page) stayed failed both before and after — unchanged, not worse.

**What didn't work — and this is the part that matters given your explicit "don't let it degrade" requirement:**
3 pages regressed by more than 0.05 char-F1:
- `as_p0334`: `0.569` → `0.194` (a serious regression — the output now degenerates into repetitive "Sample the same as above..." filler)
- `as_p0441`: `0.584` → `0.443` (moderate)
- `as_p0528`: `0.477` → **new failure** (flips to `repetition-degeneration`, a failure mode it didn't have before)

So the failure *count* looks unchanged (2 before, 2 after) only because one failure got fixed (`as_p0534`) while a different, previously-fine page broke (`as_p0528`) — it's a trade, not a clean win. The catastrophic-forgetting guard we built specifically to catch this did its job here.

**Region-crop diagnostic**: inconclusive — `layout.detect()` found no table region on either `as_p0509` or `as_p0351`, so the whole-page-vs-crop comparison couldn't run at all. That's itself informative: the layout detector isn't recognizing these dense pages as containing a "table" region, which may be part of why the model struggles on them.

**My honest read**: this isn't a clean "ship it" result. Given the explicit ask was "make sure our current model does not degrade," 3 regressed pages (one badly) means this table_ft adapter shouldn't silently replace curve_n122. Worth deciding how you want to proceed — for example, dropping the epoch count or diluting the table-heavy mix with more non-table pages could reduce this localized overfitting, but that's another GPU run.

v7's results are in — and they're **worse than v6**, not better. My dilution hypothesis was wrong. Here's the direct comparison:

| Metric | Baseline (curve_n122) | v6 (100% table mix, 6 epochs) | v7 (diluted, 3 epochs, gentler LR) |
|---|---|---|---|
| failure_rate | 0.10 (2/20) | 0.10 (2/20) | **0.25 (5/20)** — worse |
| char_f1 (successes only) | 0.489 | 0.568 | 0.576 |

That char_f1 number for v7 is misleading on its own: it's averaged only over pages that didn't fail, and v7 has *more* failures than v6, so weaker pages are dropping out of the average rather than actually improving — the aggregate number going up while failure_rate nearly triples is a red flag, not a win.

Page-by-page, v7 vs v6:
- **as_p0509** (the original target page): v6 got it to 0.249, v7 only reached 0.211 — v7 improved it *less*.
- **as_p0534**: v6 recovered this from an outright failure to f1=0.480. v7 failed to recover it at all — still failing.
- **as_p0334**: still regressed in v7 (0.569→0.266), less badly than v6's (→0.194) but still a real regression.
- **as_p0441**: regressed almost identically in both v6 and v7 (→0.443).
- **as_p0528**: fails in both v6 and v7 (0.477→failed).
- **Two brand-new failures in v7 that v6 didn't have**: `as_p0453` (0.294→failed) and `as_p0518` (0.425→failed).

So diluting the mix and going gentler didn't fix the instability — it made it broader. That suggests the problem isn't "100% table content warps the gradient," it's something more fundamentally unstable about continuing training on this small an adapter nudge, and lower LR/fewer epochs didn't compensate.

ME : 

you give The fine-tuned reader" is data/models/ocr_lora/curve_n122 as the base model but in the table_fine tune we got better char f1 so why we take The fine-tuned reader" is data/models/ocr_lora/curve_n122 for the step 29 plz explain me

AI : 

Fair question — the aggregate number really does favor `table_ft` (0.568 vs 0.489), so it's worth spelling out why "higher average" didn't win.

**The aggregate number hides which pages moved, not just how much.** `char_f1 = 0.568` is the *mean* char-F1 across the ~18 pages that didn't outright fail. A mean can go up while individual pages get meaningfully worse, as long as other pages improve enough to outweigh them — and that's exactly what happened:

| Page | curve_n122 | table_ft | Direction |
|---|---|---|---|
| as_p0509 | 0.099 | 0.249 | up |
| as_p0534 | FAILED | 0.480 | up (recovered) |
| as_p0334 | 0.569 | 0.194 | **down, badly** |
| as_p0441 | 0.584 | 0.443 | down |
| as_p0528 | 0.477 | FAILED | **down, new failure** |

Two pages got a lot better, three got worse — one of them (`as_p0528`) went from working fine to an outright repetition-degeneration failure it didn't have before. The average absorbs that trade and reports it as "improvement," but if you're the person whose query happens to land on `as_p0334`, the fine-tune made your result *worse*, not better. Aggregate metrics are exactly the thing that hides this — it's the reason `run_finetune_tables.py` was built to print per-page before/after in the first place, not just the mean.

**The explicit design goal for Stage C was non-regression, not "improve the average."** Your original ask was "make sure our current model does not degrade" — that's a per-page guarantee, not an aggregate one. `table_ft` fails that guarantee on 3 pages. It doesn't matter that the mean went up; the thing it was actually supposed to satisfy, it didn't.

**We tried to explain the regressions and couldn't.** v7 tested the obvious hypothesis (100%-table training mix warping the gradient) by diluting the mix — and it made things *worse*, with two brand-new failures. That means we genuinely don't know why `as_p0334`/`as_p0441`/`as_p0528` regress. Shipping a model with an unexplained, unpredictable failure pattern as the one used for Step 29 is risky specifically because of what Step 29 is: **the one-time TEST measurement, run exactly once against the 39 held-out pages we've never touched.** If whatever property makes `as_p0334` regress also shows up in some of those 39 pages, you find out on the one evaluation you don't get to repeat. `curve_n122`'s behavior, by contrast, is the one we actually understand — its weaknesses (radicals/big-operators, table hallucination) trace to a known, documented cause (Stage A's structural coverage gap), not a mystery.

**Small sample, real volatility.** The 0.079 aggregate gain is averaged over ~18 pages, with individual pages swinging by 0.1–0.4 in either direction. That's not a stable signal — Step 28's own point 8 already flagged batch_size=1 training variance as a real source of exactly this kind of per-page noise. A gain that size, built from swings that large, isn't something I'd bet the one-shot TEST number on.

So the choice wasn't "ignore the better number" — it was "the number that looked better doesn't mean what it appears to mean once you look at which pages moved," and Step 29 is the one place in the whole project where that distinction actually matters most.

ME : 

so in general which one will work better in most cases...as the char f1 score in not too good so is there any thing we can do for improving the char f1 score

AI :


**Which works better "in general":** neither cleanly — it depends on what you're using it for.

- Purely by the numbers, `table_ft` has a higher mean char-F1 (0.568 vs 0.489) at the same failure rate, so for a *random unseen page*, it's the statistically favored bet on average.
- But "on average" is doing a lot of work here: we don't know *what kind* of page triggers its regressions. `as_p0334`/`as_p0441`/`as_p0528` aren't obviously table pages, and v7 already showed our best guess at the cause (100%-table training mix) was wrong. An unpredictable ~15% regression rate is a bad trade for anything where a single result matters (Step 29's one-shot TEST run) — but it might be a perfectly fine trade for something like Step 30's full-book re-OCR, where quality gets judged in aggregate across ~1046 pages and a handful of worse pages barely move the needle. The one thing that *would* make `table_ft` risky even there: its regressions include full repetition-degeneration failures (garbage/empty output), not just lower scores — and a garbage chunk actively pollutes retrieval rather than just scoring low, so it's not a totally free trade even in the aggregate-corpus case.

So: `curve_n122` for anything where individual-page reliability matters (which is why it's Step 29's pick); `table_ft` is defensible for corpus-aggregate use, but only with that repetition-failure caveat in mind.

**On actually raising char-F1**, a few things grounded in what this session already found, roughly in order of how well-evidenced they are:

1. **Fix repetition-degeneration at generation time, not just detect it.** Right now a spiraling page just gets flagged as failed and contributes zero to char-F1. Tightening `no_repeat_ngram_size`/repetition penalty, or switching from greedy to beam search for generation, could convert some of those failures into real (if imperfect) output — which both lowers failure_rate *and* raises char-F1, since fewer pages get discarded from the average.
2. **Gradient accumulation to fix the batch_size=1 training variance already flagged.** Step 28 measured the *same page* swinging 0.712→0.421→0.923→0.291 across curve points — that's noise dominating signal, not real learning. This was flagged and never acted on. Stabilizing training could raise the reliable floor, not just the lucky-seed ceiling.
3. **Targeted annotation for radicals/big-operators**, the same way Step 18b targeted specific chapters. Stage A structurally has zero `\sqrt`/`\sum`/`\int`/`\prod` examples — Stage B's 122 pages are the only exposure, likely thin. More train pages chosen specifically for these constructs (not random pages) is the same evidence-driven approach that worked for the 105→122 expansion.
4. **Route per-region instead of per-page: `curve_n122` normally, `table_ft` only inside a detected table region.** This is actually the more interesting idea, and it's already half-built — `run_finetune_tables.py`'s region-crop diagnostic tried exactly this, but `layout.detect()` didn't recognize `as_p0509`/`as_p0351` as containing a table region at all, so it couldn't even run. If the layout detector's table-region recall improves first, this sidesteps the whole regression problem entirely — you'd get `table_ft`'s table gains without ever applying it to the pages it's bad at, since it would only ever touch cropped table regions.

Of these, #4 is the one I'd actually chase first if there's appetite for more work — it directly resolves the tension this whole conversation has been about, rather than trading one failure mode for another. But all four are real GPU/annotation time, not free — want me to scope any of them out further, or is this enough to decide whether it's worth pursuing before Step 29/30?

Both are feasible, but I found some real constraints that change the plan. Here's what I checked:

**Repetition-degeneration fix — cheaper than I first framed it, and there's a timing issue worth acting on now.**

Correcting myself from earlier: `no_repeat_ngram_size` was already tried and explicitly rejected back at Step 18b — A&S legitimately repeats subscripts and table rows (a column of `0`s, runs of `\frac{1}{2}`), and that hard constraint would corrupt those. The soft `repetition_penalty=1.1` currently in `vision/ocr.py` is already the considered middle ground, not an oversight. What's genuinely untried: **beam search** — `generate()` currently has no `num_beams` set, so it's plain greedy decoding, which is exactly the decoding strategy most prone to getting stuck in a repetition spiral once it commits to a bad token. Trying `num_beams=3-4` is a real, cheap, unexplored lever.

The timing issue: I checked `CHANGELOG.md` — **Step 29 hasn't run yet** (still `[ ]`, owned by S2). If you fold this generation-parameter change into Step 30 (which runs *after* Step 29 per `plan.md`'s dependency), Step 29's TEST numbers would be measured with the *old* generation config, while Step 30's shipped index would use the *new* one — a real mismatch between what got reported and what's actually indexed. Since Step 29 hasn't run yet, there's still a clean window: validate the beam-search/penalty change now (cheap — no retraining, just re-running eval on the already-trained `curve_n122` adapter with different generation params), and if it helps, land it in `vision/ocr.py` and tell S2 *before* they run Step 29, so both steps end up using the same, better reader. That's worth coordinating on now rather than quietly changing it mid-Step-30.

**Per-region routing — bigger than it looked, with an unresolved prerequisite.**

I read the actual pipeline (`vision/ocr.py`'s `transcribe()`): it's whole-page-first by deliberate design (Step 11's own finding — Nougat was trained on whole pages, and whole-page context is more accurate, not just faster). Per-region processing *does* already exist, but only as a fallback retry when whole-page decoding fails outright — there's no existing concept of "always route table-kind regions to a different adapter." Building that means: `Reader` holding two adapters loaded simultaneously, new routing logic that breaks from the whole-page default specifically for table regions, and critically — **Stage C's own diagnostic already found `layout.detect()` finds zero table regions on the two pages that motivated this whole line of work.** If that's true more broadly, not just on those two pages, the routing infrastructure would have very little to actually route to.

Before touching any of that: check, cheaply and without GPU, how often `layout.detect()` actually finds table regions across a wider sample (the 26 table-dense train pages + the 20 val pages) — not just the 2 known-bad ones. That answers "is this worth building" before spending engineering time on it, and it doesn't risk Step 30's actual required deliverable (the full 1046-page re-OCR + index).

Want me to run that table-region-recall check now? It's free (no GPU, local code + already-materialized page images) and tells us fast whether region-routing is worth pursuing at all before Step 30.

The hybrid ran clean (22 table-region crops generated, no crash) — and the results are genuinely mixed, not a clean win. Here's the honest picture:

**Aggregate: char-F1 best of the three (0.584), but failure_rate is worse, not better** — 0.20 (4/20 pages) vs 0.10 for both `curve_n122` alone and `table_ft` whole-page alone.

**Where the hypothesis held, convincingly:**

| Page | curve_n122 | table_ft (whole-page) | hybrid (region-routed) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `as_p0509` (the original motivating page) | 0.099 | 0.249 | **0.525** |
| `as_p0334` (v6's worst regression) | 0.569 | 0.194 | **0.569** (fully restored) |
| `as_p0441` (v6 regression) | 0.584 | 0.443 | **0.668** (beats both) |
| `as_p0528` (v6's new failure) | 0.477 | FAILED | **0.416** (failure avoided, small residual dip) |

`as_p0334` has no detected table region, so `table_ft` was never applied to it under routing — it passed through untouched and its regression vanished entirely, exactly as the theory predicted. `as_p0509` more than doubled `table_ft`'s own whole-page gain, since the crop-only generation let the model focus purely on the table without whole-page distraction. Three other non-tracked pages with table regions (`as_p0350`, `as_p0507`, `as_p0564`) also beat both baselines.

**Where it didn't hold:**

- **`as_p0534`**: `table_ft` whole-page had recovered this from an outright failure to `0.480`. Under routing, it's **still failed**. This page does have a detected table region, so `table_ft` did run on its table crop — meaning whatever let whole-page `table_ft` recover this page wasn't really about the table content specifically, and confining it to just the crop lost that benefit.
- **Two brand-new failures neither pure approach had**: `as_p0459` → `nougat-missing-page-marker`, `as_p0518` → `repetition-degeneration`. Neither `curve_n122` nor `table_ft` whole-page failed on these pages at all.
- `as_p0563` also picked up a smaller new regression (0.824/0.832 → 0.727).

**Root cause of the new failures is identifiable, not mysterious.** The merge step (`_split_markdown_to_regions`) is explicitly documented in the codebase as "an approximation, not an alignment" — it splits `curve_n122`'s whole-page text on blank lines and pairs blocks with regions by *position*, not real correspondence. Splicing a freshly-generated table crop into that approximate split evidently creates a bad seam on at least 2 pages, tripping failure detectors neither standalone approach ever triggered.

**Verdict: the hypothesis is partially confirmed, not fully.** The core idea — table-crop generation genuinely helps where it applies — is real and strong (as_p0509, as_p0334, as_p0441 are convincing). But the current splice mechanism is a second source of risk on top of the one it was meant to fix, and net failure count went *up* (2→4), not down. This isn't ready to fold into Step 30 as-is; the merge/recombination step would need real work (a proper region-to-text alignment, not a position-based approximation) before it's trustworthy at full-book scale. Everything from this check stayed local/scratch — `KAGGLE/region_routing_check/` is uncommitted and I haven't touched anything that affects Step 29/30's actual deliverables.

To directly answer your question: **yes — everything needed to decide is already sitting in the data from the two Kaggle runs. No new training required.** Here's the head-to-head, `v6` vs `routing`, not vs baseline this time:

| | v6 wins | routing wins | tie |
|---|---|---|---|
| count | 8 pages | 9 pages | 3 pages |

Roughly even by raw count — but the *type* of win differs in a way that matters:

- **v6's wins include rescuing 3 pages routing fails on entirely** (`as_p0459`, `as_p0518`, `as_p0534`) — a failed page contributes literally nothing, which the project's own metric convention (failure rate as "its own headline number, not a footnote," established back at Step 16/29) treats as categorically worse than a low score.
- **Routing's wins are real score gains on pages that still produce output either way** (`as_p0334` +0.375, `as_p0509` +0.276, `as_p0441` +0.225) — genuine quality improvements, not failure-avoidance.

So the decision rule the project has used everywhere else — failure rate first, aggregate score second — already tells you the answer without needing another GPU run: **v6 is the safer pick right now**, because routing's net failure count is worse (4 vs 2), even though its per-success quality is often better.

The useful part for later: since the root cause traces to the splice code (`_split_markdown_to_regions`), not the adapters, **fixing this doesn't require retraining either** — it's a software fix (a real region-to-text alignment instead of a blank-line position guess) followed by re-running the *same* already-trained `curve_n122`/`table_ft` adapters through the *same* validation script. **"v6" is a Kaggle kernel push number, not a model.** It refers to the 6th time I pushed the Stage C notebook, which was the first one that ran successfully end-to-end. The actual model artifact it produced is `data/models/ocr_lora/table_ft/`.

**`table_ft` is built on top of `curve_n122`, not trained from scratch.** The layering is:

1. **`curve_n122`** — Step 28's own fine-tune: Stage A (695 degraded NIST formula pairs) → Stage B (122 A&S train pages, whole pages — formulas, prose, *and* whatever tables happen to appear on those pages). This is the "normal" fine-tuned reader, general-purpose, not table-specialized.
2. **`table_ft`** — takes `curve_n122` as its *starting point* and continues fine-tuning it further, specifically on: 9 real NIST table pairs + 26 A&S pages picked because they're table-dense. It doesn't relearn formulas/prose from scratch — it inherits everything `curve_n122` already knows and nudges it further toward tables.

So `table_ft` **is** fine-tuned on both — it's not "just tables" replacing the normal model, it's the normal model (`curve_n122`) plus an additional table-focused continuation pass. That's exactly why the whole investigation this session has been about: because it inherits everything, table_ft mostly behaves like `curve_n122` on non-table pages — except on the 3 pages where that continuation pass caused a regression, which is the actual problem we've been diagnosing.

The region-routing experiment we just validated was a *third* thing again — not a different training run, just the two *existing* adapters (`curve_n122` and `table_ft`) applied selectively per-region on the same 20 pages, no new fine-tuning involved.

Two things to answer — I should clarify the first question too, since I moved straight to the fix without addressing it.

**What "v6" actually is**: `v6` was just the Kaggle kernel *version number* for the push that produced the kept adapter — the file itself lives at `data/models/ocr_lora/table_ft/`. It is **not** the "normal" version. `curve_n122` is the normal one: Step 28's standard 2-stage curriculum (Stage A = degraded NIST formula pairs, Stage B = 122 A&S train pages), with no table-specific focus at all. `table_ft` (= v6's output) is a *further* continuation fine-tune starting from `curve_n122`, trained specifically on 9 real NIST table pairs + 26 table-dense A&S pages — so it's fine-tuned on tables *in addition to* everything `curve_n122` already knows, not instead of it.

**Now the fix results — and they're clean.** All 3 previously-failed pages are fixed:

| Page | curve_n122 | table_ft (v6) | hybrid, before fix | hybrid, after fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `as_p0459` | 0.256 | 0.239 | FAILED | **0.256** |
| `as_p0518` | 0.425 | 0.379 | FAILED | **0.425** |
| `as_p0534` | FAILED | 0.480 | FAILED | **0.480** |

Turns out the fix for `as_p0459`/`as_p0518` didn't even need fresh crop generation — every one of their table-region "original chunks" was empty (the position-approximation had run out of real text blocks), so Fix 3 correctly skipped substitution entirely and the page just falls back to `curve_n122`'s already-fine text, unchanged. `as_p0534` correctly falls back to `table_ft`'s cached whole-page result.

**Recomputing the full 20-page aggregate with these 3 pages fixed** (the other 17 are unchanged from the original hybrid run):

| | failure_rate | char_f1 |
|---|---|---|
| `curve_n122` (normal) | 0.10 | 0.489 |
| `table_ft` / v6 (whole-page) | 0.10 | 0.568 |
| hybrid, original (buggy splice) | 0.20 | 0.584 |
| **hybrid, fixed** | **0.05** | **~0.553** |

This is now a genuine, clean win — **half the failure rate of both baselines** (1/20 vs 2/20 each), and char-F1 well above `curve_n122` and close to `table_ft`'s. Given the project's own established rule (failure rate is the headline metric, more important than aggregate score), the fixed routing approach is now the best of the three, decided entirely from data already in hand — no retraining, and this last check didn't even need the GPU.

AI : 

**Yes — you should depend on Step 29 exactly as it was measured, and Step 30 must use `curve_n122`, not the routing approach.** Here's why this isn't really a close call:

**Step 29 is explicitly a one-time measurement, by design.** The plan's own text says it directly: *"This is the only time the 39 test pages are used. Every decision was already made on validation."* That's not a formality — it's the entire point of having a held-out test set. If you now redo Step 29 with a different reader (the routing approach) because it looked better, you've used the test set to *pick* between readers, which quietly turns it into a second validation set. The number stops being an honest, unbiased estimate the moment you do that, even if the new number looks better.

**Step 30's whole job is to use the SAME reader Step 29 already measured.** That's the logical chain the two steps form together: Step 29 proves how good "the fine-tuned reader" is on held-out data, Step 30 then uses that exact reader to re-OCR the full book, so the shipped index's actual quality matches the number in your report. If Step 30 quietly swapped in the routing hybrid instead, the index you ship would be running on a reader Step 29 never actually measured — the report's char-F1/failure-rate table would describe a different system than the one that built the index. That breaks the project's own "grounding gate" (*"numbers with no notebook evidence score zero"*) in spirit, even if no single number is technically false.

**Practically**: check with your teammate what adapter they actually pointed at for Step 29 — I added an explicit note to `plan.md`'s Step 29 section earlier saying "use `curve_n122`, not `table_ft`," specifically so this wouldn't come up. **Where does the routing work go, then?** It doesn't disappear — it's genuinely good evidence (fixed hybrid beat both `curve_n122` and `table_ft` on the 20 val pages, half the failure rate). > _ran: `Bash(cd "d:\Project A1\Project A1\doc-agent-1" && git ls-tree -r main --name-only -- data/ocr | head -20; echo "---count---"; git ls-tree -r main --name-on)`_

Found a real, serious correctness bug — not a crash, but worse: it would silently succeed while producing the wrong result. Let me explain and fix it before launching anything further.

**The bug**: `data/ocr/` already has **744 committed `.mmd` files** on `main`, left over from Step 16/18b's *pretrained-baseline* reader run (deliberately committed as that step's own real deliverable). `ocr.transcribe()`'s resumability cache (`if mmd_path.exists(): skip re-running the model`) doesn't distinguish *which reader* produced a cached file — it just sees a file and reuses it. That's exactly why 4 of the 5 smoke pages were "cached" instead of freshly transcribed: they already had baseline-reader `.mmd` files sitting in the clone. If I'd launched the full run as-is, it would have silently kept ~744 pages' worth of *old, worse baseline OCR* and only actually applied the new fine-tuned + region-routing reader to the ~300 pages that happened to have no prior transcript — completely defeating Step 30's purpose, and I wouldn't have found out until after a ~12-hour run completed "successfully."

**The fix**: on a genuine `FRESH_START`, the notebook needs to clear the cloned `data/ocr/` before running — Step 30 is supposed to *replace* the baseline transcripts with better ones, not silently inherit them. A real resume (`FRESH_START=False`) should still reseed from a *previous Step 30 push's own* checkpoint, which is already correct since that path never touches git's old baseline files.

Your plan.md edits are not in this repo. plan.md lives at D:\Project A1\Project A1\plan.md, one level above doc-agent-1, and git doesn't track it.
